========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling… not dramatic exactly, not like some sudden collapse, but there’s this steady, stubborn sense that the best version of my life is already behind me. And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.

It’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.

I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to do, and I wouldn’t say I get absolutely nothing out of them. It’s not as simple as “I used to enjoy things and now I don’t.”

I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.

It’s not as simple as “I used to enjoy things and now I don’t.”\n\nBut if you’re asking whether they feel the same as they used to… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll start something and think, well, I should be enjoying this more than I am. And then I get caught up judging the experience instead of just having it, if that makes sense. I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.

I still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.

So… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.

I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 65 / not_in_workforce / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Not really… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.

I suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.

There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.

I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.

I haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.

So… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.

And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. I tie it pretty clearly to a hiking injury I had — that was about three years and three months ago, I think. It wasn’t like one day before that I was perfectly fine and the next day everything changed, but that’s when this whole shift really began, and it just never fully lifted afterward. Since then it’s been more of a long, continuous thing than a passing bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to do, and I wouldn’t say I get absolutely nothing out of them. It’s not as simple as “I used to enjoy things and now I don’t.”\n\nBut if you’re asking whether they feel the same as they used to… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll start something and think, well, I should be enjoying this more than I am. And then I get caught up judging the experience instead of just having it, if that makes sense. I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.

But I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.

So… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.

My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.

What’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.

So I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling… not dramatic exactly, not like some sudden collapse, but there’s this steady, stubborn sense that the best version of my life is already behind me. And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. I tie it pretty clearly to a hiking injury I had — that was about three years and three months ago, I think. Since then it’s been more of a long, continuous thing than a passing bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed or hopeless, with some time in between where you felt normal or close to normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.\n\nWhat’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Yes… I think so. If I’m being honest, this has been going on since that hiking injury a little over three years ago. I tend to mark time from that because it’s when things started feeling different and didn’t really go back to the way they were.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling… not dramatic exactly, not like some sudden collapse, but there’s this steady, stubborn sense that the best version of my life is already behind me. And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. I tie it pretty clearly to a hiking injury I had — that was about three years and three months ago, I think. It wasn’t like one day before that I was perfectly fine and the next day everything changed, but that’s when this whole shift really began, and it just never fully lifted afterward. Since then it’s been more of a long, continuous thing than a passing bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to do, and I wouldn’t say I get absolutely nothing out of them. It’s not as simple as “I used to enjoy things and now I don’t.”\n\nBut if you’re asking whether they feel the same as they used to… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll start something and think, well, I should be enjoying this more than I am. And then I get caught up judging the experience instead of just having it, if that makes sense. I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed or hopeless, with some time in between where you felt normal or close to normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.\n\nWhat’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little to no improvement, for two years or more since it started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If I’m being honest, this has been going on since that hiking injury a little over three years ago. I tend to mark time from that because it’s when things started feeling different and didn’t really go back to the way they were.\n\nSo in terms of your question about two years or more, yes — it’s been longer than that. About three years and three months now, give or take. It hasn’t felt like there was a real stretch where it lifted completely or where I thought, “Okay, that’s behind me now.” It’s more like it’s just been there in the background ever since.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m not sure I’d say they no longer do. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but that wording makes it sound like the pleasure has completely disappeared, and I don’t think that’s quite right for me.

There are still things I can enjoy, at least somewhat. It’s more that I sometimes don’t feel as drawn toward them, or the enjoyment feels less immediate than I remember. Like I might know, intellectually, “I usually like this,” but actually getting myself to do it can feel less natural. Then once I’m in it, I may still get something out of it.

So… no, I wouldn’t say things that used to give me pleasure no longer do. It’s more complicated than that.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling… not dramatic exactly, not like some sudden collapse, but there’s this steady, stubborn sense that the best version of my life is already behind me. And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. I tie it pretty clearly to a hiking injury I had — that was about three years and three months ago, I think. Since then it’s been more of a long, continuous thing than a passing bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed or hopeless, with some time in between where you felt normal or close to normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.\n\nWhat’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little to no improvement, for two years or more since it started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If I’m being honest, this has been going on since that hiking injury a little over three years ago. I tend to mark time from that because it’s when things started feeling different and didn’t really go back to the way they were.\n\nSo in terms of your question about two years or more, yes — it’s been longer than that. About three years and three months now, give or take. It hasn’t felt like there was a real stretch where it lifted completely or where I thought, “Okay, that’s behind me now.” It’s more like it’s just been there in the background ever since.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure no longer do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they no longer do. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but that wording makes it sound like the pleasure has completely disappeared, and I don’t think that’s quite right for me.\n\nThere are still things I can enjoy, at least somewhat. It’s more that I sometimes don’t feel as drawn toward them, or the enjoyment feels less immediate than I remember. Like I might know, intellectually, “I usually like this,” but actually getting myself to do it can feel less natural. Then once I’m in it, I may still get something out of it.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things that used to give me pleasure no longer do. It’s more complicated than that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think that’s fair to say. Not necessarily in an obvious, falling-apart sort of way. I’m not sitting there crying all day, and I can still have conversations, do what I’m doing, be in the studio, sit at dinner with my wife and talk about ordinary things.

I may be doing something normal, even something I care about, and there’s still this heaviness or flatness running in the background.

As far as “most of the day,” I’d say yes, more often than not. It’s not always at the front of my mind every minute, but it’s present enough that I don’t feel like I get many truly clear days where it’s just gone.

I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I had anything before the hiking injury that was quite like this, at least not in the same steady, day-after-day way.

I mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, like anyone has — times when I was discouraged or unhappy or under stress — but I don’t remember this same kind of ongoing low background feeling being there for such a long time. Before that injury, if I felt down, it tended to feel more connected to something specific, and then it would pass or loosen up after a while.

So… no, I don’t think there were other periods like this one before then. Not that I can clearly identify.

Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed or hopeless, with some time in between where you felt normal or close to normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.\n\nWhat’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little to no improvement, for two years or more since it started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If I’m being honest, this has been going on since that hiking injury a little over three years ago. I tend to mark time from that because it’s when things started feeling different and didn’t really go back to the way they were.\n\nSo in terms of your question about two years or more, yes — it’s been longer than that. About three years and three months now, give or take. It hasn’t felt like there was a real stretch where it lifted completely or where I thought, “Okay, that’s behind me now.” It’s more like it’s just been there in the background ever since.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure no longer do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they no longer do. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but that wording makes it sound like the pleasure has completely disappeared, and I don’t think that’s quite right for me.\n\nThere are still things I can enjoy, at least somewhat. It’s more that I sometimes don’t feel as drawn toward them, or the enjoyment feels less immediate than I remember. Like I might know, intellectually, “I usually like this,” but actually getting myself to do it can feel less natural. Then once I’m in it, I may still get something out of it.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things that used to give me pleasure no longer do. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of depression or hopelessness been present for most of the day, most days, since they started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. Not necessarily in an obvious, falling-apart sort of way. I’m not sitting there crying all day, and I can still have conversations, do what I’m doing, be in the studio, sit at dinner with my wife and talk about ordinary things.\n\nBut most days there’s this low, gray tone underneath everything. That’s the best way I can put it. It’s like the emotional lighting in the room is dimmer, even when nothing especially bad is happening in that moment. I may be doing something normal, even something I care about, and there’s still this heaviness or flatness running in the background.\n\nAs far as “most of the day,” I’d say yes, more often than not. It’s not always at the front of my mind every minute, but it’s present enough that I don’t feel like I get many truly clear days where it’s just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced other periods of depression like this one before the hiking injury?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I had anything before the hiking injury that was quite like this, at least not in the same steady, day-after-day way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, like anyone has — times when I was discouraged or unhappy or under stress — but I don’t remember this same kind of ongoing low background feeling being there for such a long time. Before that injury, if I felt down, it tended to feel more connected to something specific, and then it would pass or loosen up after a while.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there were other periods like this one before then. Not that I can clearly identify.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling… not dramatic exactly, not like some sudden collapse, but there’s this steady, stubborn sense that the best version of my life is already behind me. And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. I tie it pretty clearly to a hiking injury I had — that was about three years and three months ago, I think. It wasn’t like one day before that I was perfectly fine and the next day everything changed, but that’s when this whole shift really began, and it just never fully lifted afterward. Since then it’s been more of a long, continuous thing than a passing bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to do, and I wouldn’t say I get absolutely nothing out of them. It’s not as simple as “I used to enjoy things and now I don’t.”\n\nBut if you’re asking whether they feel the same as they used to… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll start something and think, well, I should be enjoying this more than I am. And then I get caught up judging the experience instead of just having it, if that makes sense. I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed or hopeless, with some time in between where you felt normal or close to normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.\n\nWhat’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little to no improvement, for two years or more since it started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If I’m being honest, this has been going on since that hiking injury a little over three years ago. I tend to mark time from that because it’s when things started feeling different and didn’t really go back to the way they were.\n\nSo in terms of your question about two years or more, yes — it’s been longer than that. About three years and three months now, give or take. It hasn’t felt like there was a real stretch where it lifted completely or where I thought, “Okay, that’s behind me now.” It’s more like it’s just been there in the background ever since.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure no longer do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they no longer do. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but that wording makes it sound like the pleasure has completely disappeared, and I don’t think that’s quite right for me.\n\nThere are still things I can enjoy, at least somewhat. It’s more that I sometimes don’t feel as drawn toward them, or the enjoyment feels less immediate than I remember. Like I might know, intellectually, “I usually like this,” but actually getting myself to do it can feel less natural. Then once I’m in it, I may still get something out of it.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things that used to give me pleasure no longer do. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of depression or hopelessness been present for most of the day, most days, since they started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. Not necessarily in an obvious, falling-apart sort of way. I’m not sitting there crying all day, and I can still have conversations, do what I’m doing, be in the studio, sit at dinner with my wife and talk about ordinary things.\n\nBut most days there’s this low, gray tone underneath everything. That’s the best way I can put it. It’s like the emotional lighting in the room is dimmer, even when nothing especially bad is happening in that moment. I may be doing something normal, even something I care about, and there’s still this heaviness or flatness running in the background.\n\nAs far as “most of the day,” I’d say yes, more often than not. It’s not always at the front of my mind every minute, but it’s present enough that I don’t feel like I get many truly clear days where it’s just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced other periods of depression like this one before the hiking injury?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I had anything before the hiking injury that was quite like this, at least not in the same steady, day-after-day way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, like anyone has — times when I was discouraged or unhappy or under stress — but I don’t remember this same kind of ongoing low background feeling being there for such a long time. Before that injury, if I felt down, it tended to feel more connected to something specific, and then it would pass or loosen up after a while.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there were other periods like this one before then. Not that I can clearly identify.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt depressed or hopeless for an extended period before the hiking injury?\n\nPatient: No, not really — at least not in the way this has been. This really seems to have started after the hiking injury, and it’s been going on since then.\n\nI suppose if I count it out, it’s been a little over three years now… about three years and three months, something like that. Which sounds strange to say out loud, because day to day it doesn’t always feel like I’m measuring it, but when I step back, yes, that’s how long it’s been. Before that, I don’t think I had an extended period like this.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Not necessarily in an obvious, falling-apart sort of way. That’s the best way I can put it. It’s like the emotional lighting in the room is dimmer, even when nothing especially bad is happening in that moment. I may be doing something normal, even something I care about, and there’s still this heaviness or flatness running in the background.\n\nAs far as “most of the day,” I’d say yes, more often than not. It’s not always at the front of my mind every minute, but it’s present enough that I don’t feel like I get many truly clear days where it’s just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced other periods of depression like this one before the hiking injury?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I had anything before the hiking injury that was quite like this, at least not in the same steady, day-after-day way.\n\nI mean, I’ve had difficult stretches in life, like anyone has — times when I was discouraged or unhappy or under stress — but I don’t remember this same kind of ongoing low background feeling being there for such a long time. Before that injury, if I felt down, it tended to feel more connected to something specific, and then it would pass or loosen up after a while.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think there were other periods like this one before then. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. Not two months in a row where it was completely gone.

There may have been better weeks, or stretches where I was distracted or functioning more smoothly, but if you mean really free of that low feeling — like it lifted and stayed lifted — then no. Since the hiking injury, it’s more or less been there the whole time, in some form. And that’s a little over three years now… about three years and three months, I think. It’s hard to say that precisely, but no, I can’t point to a two-month period where I felt clear of it.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.1, F33.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been feeling… not dramatic exactly, not like some sudden collapse, but there’s this steady, stubborn sense that the best version of my life is already behind me. And I keep thinking that no matter how much I organize myself or make plans or try to be disciplined about things, I can’t quite get back to feeling like myself.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because from the outside it may not look like much has changed, but inside it feels like I’ve lost some basic confidence that things can really turn around. I’m not saying I’ve given up entirely, but there’s this quiet hopelessness that’s been sitting there, and I thought maybe I should talk to someone before it becomes even more fixed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over three years now. I tie it pretty clearly to a hiking injury I had — that was about three years and three months ago, I think. It wasn’t like one day before that I was perfectly fine and the next day everything changed, but that’s when this whole shift really began, and it just never fully lifted afterward. Since then it’s been more of a long, continuous thing than a passing bad spell.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself enjoying activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, honestly. I mean, I still do some of the things I used to do, and I wouldn’t say I get absolutely nothing out of them. It’s not as simple as “I used to enjoy things and now I don’t.”\n\nBut if you’re asking whether they feel the same as they used to… I don’t know. Sometimes I’ll start something and think, well, I should be enjoying this more than I am. And then I get caught up judging the experience instead of just having it, if that makes sense. I still have moments where something is pleasant, but it can feel a little muted or harder to settle into.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two weeks where you felt consistently depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think so. Though “period” makes it sound more contained than it feels. For me it hasn’t been a neat two-week stretch where I could point to a beginning and an end. It’s more that, most days, I’m not crying or falling apart or anything dramatic like that, but there’s this low, gray tone running underneath everything.\n\nEven when I’m doing things that should feel normal — being in the studio, or sitting across from my wife at dinner — it’s there in the background. Not always loud, but present. So yes, if the question is whether I’ve had at least two weeks of feeling depressed or hopeless consistently, then yes. It’s been that way for much longer than two weeks.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities or things you used to care about?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it a clean “lost interest,” exactly. It’s more complicated than that for me.\n\nI still care about things, at least intellectually. I still know what used to matter to me, and I still make myself do some of it. But the pull toward those things feels weaker sometimes. Like I may think, “I should go do that,” or “That used to make me feel connected to myself,” but then actually getting into it feels harder, or less immediate.\n\nSo… yes and no, I suppose. I haven’t stopped caring altogether. It’s not like everything became meaningless overnight. But there’s a kind of distance from things that used to come more naturally. I find myself observing my own reaction instead of just being absorbed in it. And that makes it hard to tell whether I’ve lost interest, or whether I’m just not able to access the interest in the same way.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really… at least, not that I’ve noticed in any clear way. My weight has always moved around a little bit, you know, depending on the season or how much attention I’m paying to meals, but I can’t point to some major gain or loss that started when I began feeling this way.\n\nI suppose I don’t weigh myself as regularly as I used to, so it’s possible there have been small changes I’m not tracking. But my clothes fit more or less the same, and no one’s commented on it. So I’d say no significant change, as far as I can tell.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods where you felt better in between times when you felt depressed or hopeless?\n\nPatient: There have been better stretches, yes… but I’m not sure I’d describe them as being completely “between” episodes, if that makes sense. It’s more like the volume turns down for a while rather than the whole thing disappearing.\n\nThis all really started after the hiking injury a little over three years ago — about three years and three months now, if I’m counting right. Since then, there have been days or maybe short stretches where I feel lighter, or more like myself, and I think, “Oh, maybe this is lifting.” But it doesn’t feel like I return all the way to some clear normal baseline before it comes back. It’s more that the grayness recedes a bit and then settles in again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can give you a neat answer to that. I mean, sleep has always been a little variable for me, so it’s hard to say what is “because of this” versus just being older, or having an off week, or not keeping the most regular schedule.\n\nI haven’t noticed some dramatic change where I suddenly stopped sleeping or started sleeping all day. It’s more that if there have been changes, they’re subtle enough that I don’t have a clear before-and-after picture. Some nights feel more restless than others, but I don’t know that I’d call it a definite pattern.\n\nSo… I guess my honest answer is that I’m not sure. I haven’t tracked it closely enough to say there’s been a clear change in my sleep since all this started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you no longer derive pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “no longer derive pleasure,” exactly. That sounds a little more absolute than what I experience. There are things I used to enjoy that still register as enjoyable, at least in moments. If I’m doing something pleasant, I can recognize it as pleasant.\n\nBut I guess I don’t always feel the same pull toward things, or the same ease getting into them. Sometimes I’ll think, “I should do that, I usually like that,” and then it feels like more effort than it should. But once I’m there, or once I get started, it’s not necessarily empty.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say pleasure is gone. Maybe it’s more muted sometimes, but I’m not sure if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you had more than one period in your life where you felt depressed or hopeless, with some time in between where you felt normal or close to normal?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, honestly. I mean, looking back over my life, I’ve certainly had difficult times where I felt low or discouraged, like anyone does, but I don’t know that I can neatly separate them into distinct periods of being depressed, then feeling normal, then another clear period later.\n\nWhat’s hard for me is that “normal or close to normal” is a little slippery. I can remember times when I was functioning better, or felt more like myself, but whether that counts as a true break in between… I don’t know. I don’t have it mapped out that cleanly in my head.\n\nSo I guess my answer is: I’m not sure. I wouldn’t confidently say I’ve had multiple separate episodes with clear normal stretches in between. It feels more muddled than that when I try to look back.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this way, with little to no improvement, for two years or more since it started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If I’m being honest, this has been going on since that hiking injury a little over three years ago. I tend to mark time from that because it’s when things started feeling different and didn’t really go back to the way they were.\n\nSo in terms of your question about two years or more, yes — it’s been longer than that. About three years and three months now, give or take. It hasn’t felt like there was a real stretch where it lifted completely or where I thought, “Okay, that’s behind me now.” It’s more like it’s just been there in the background ever since.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like things that used to give you pleasure no longer do?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say they no longer do. I know I’m sort of splitting hairs, but that wording makes it sound like the pleasure has completely disappeared, and I don’t think that’s quite right for me.\n\nThere are still things I can enjoy, at least somewhat. It’s more that I sometimes don’t feel as drawn toward them, or the enjoyment feels less immediate than I remember. Like I might know, intellectually, “I usually like this,” but actually getting myself to do it can feel less natural. Then once I’m in it, I may still get something out of it.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say things that used to give me pleasure no longer do. It’s more complicated than that.\n\nDoctor: Have your feelings of depression or hopelessness been present for most of the day, most days, since they started?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s fair to say. Not necessarily in an obvious, falling-apart sort of way. I may be doing something normal, even something I care about, and there’s still this heaviness or flatness running in the background.\n\nAs far as “most of the day,” I’d say yes, more often than not. It’s not always at the front of my mind every minute, but it’s present enough that I don’t feel like I get many truly clear days where it’s just gone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced other periods of depression like this one before the hiking injury?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I have. It’s not that I’m completely unable to do things — I still get up, I still go shopping, I still putter around with little projects at home. So from the outside I probably look like I’m functioning reasonably well.

But the energy feels different than it used to. Small things take more out of me now. Like if I’m setting up for a mentoring session, even just getting materials together or preparing myself mentally, afterward I can feel strangely worn down. Or if I go to a community event and walk around, talk to people a bit, nothing especially strenuous, I can come home feeling like I’ve done something much bigger.

And the odd part is that resting doesn’t always reset it. I might lie down or take a nap, and it helps a little maybe, but it doesn’t really fix that worn-out feeling. It’s more like I’m moving through the day with a smaller battery than I used to have.
